7.4   One-step matrix zeroing, row-zeroed matrix: Proof of Lemma 7.3
     The proof is an adaption of the proof of [25, Lemma 4.18]. Again, we relate the ‘type change’ events in question
to the existence of proper relations. More precisely, for i ∈ [n], we will show that on a reasonably likely event Sn (i),

‘average’ probability of Sn (i)c over i ∈ [n]. This requires the bulk of the work. Third, we prove the implication (7.24).

Proof of implication (7.24) by contraposition. Observe that if supp(An,s (, i)) = ∅, given Pn,s , clearly the premise of
(7.24) is not satisfied and there is nothing to prove. We thus assume supp(An,s (, i)) ̸= ∅. Suppose that Sn (i) holds
and that supp(An,s (, i)) is neither a proper relation of An,s Ji; iK[θ]T nor of An,s+1/n Ji; iK[θ]T . On Sn (i),
                       supp(An,s (, i)) = supp(An,s [θ]Ji;K(, i)) = supp(An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K(, i)),
so that we may regard supp(An,s (, i)) as the set of non-zero entries of the ith column in any of these matrices, as this
is unaffected by the perturbation and replacing rows i, νs+1/n by a zero rows and column νs+1/n by a zero column.
Moreover, again on Sn (i),
                                                                                                                  
An,s Ji; iK[θ] = An,s [θ]Ji; iK = (An,s [θ]Ji;K) J; iK, An,s+1/n Ji; iK[θ] = An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iK = An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K J; iK,
so that the assumption on proper relations concerns the ith column of An,s [θ]Ji;K and An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K, respectively.
Since the support may still form a (non-proper) relation, we distinguish four cases:
Case 1: supp(An,s (, i)) has no representation in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT and no representation in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT .
          As there is no representation of the non-zero entries of the ith column of An,s [θ]Ji;K in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT ,
          this column cannot lie in the column space of An,s [θ]Ji; iK. Thus, by the second equivalence in (3.4), i
          is frozen in An,s [θ]Ji;K.
                                    Applying the same reasoning to An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K yields i ∈ F (An,s [θ]Ji;K) ∩
          F An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K .
Case 2: supp(An,s (, i)) has a representation both in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT and in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT .
        Since we also assume that supp(An,s (, i)) is not a proper relation in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT and in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT ,
        all variables in supp(An,s (, i)) are frozen both in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT and in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT . In particular,
        column i of An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K is contained in the column space of An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iK and column i of An,s [θ]Ji;K
        is contained in the column space of An,s [θ]Ji; iK. Thus, by the second equivalence in (3.4), i is neither frozen
        in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K nor in An,s [θ]Ji;K.
Case 3: supp(An,s (, i)) has a representation in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT , but none in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT .
        As in Case 2, all variables in supp(An,s (, i)) must be frozen in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT , but there is a variable
        that is not frozen in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT . However, this cannot happen on Sn,2 (i).
Case 4: supp(An,s (, i)) has a representation in An,s [θ]Ji; iKT , but none in An,s+1/n [θ]Ji; iKT .
        By the same reasoning as in Case 3, this cannot happen on Sn,2 (i).

8   Conditional degree of the last awakened vertex in CMn,Js+1/nK
     Recall that the vector ζs+1/n represents the type distribution with respect to the perturbed adjacency matrix
An,s+1/n [θ], which is determined by CMn,Js+1/nK and the perturbation Θ. In order to apply Proposition 6.3 to derive
the type fixed-point equations, the next step is to estimate P(νs+1/n ∈ W (An,s [θ]) | ζs+1/n ) from (6.8).
     The present section prepares this estimate: To determine the type distribution of νs+1/n in An,s [θ] given ζs+1/n ,
we first compute the current degree distribution of νs+1/n in CMn,JsK conditionally on CMn,Js+1/nK . Let
                                          K
                                         X ℓ − 1  λ(ts )e2ts k                      ℓ−k−1

               qk := qk (s) = λ(ts )           ℓ                         1−                      e−ℓts pℓ         (8.1)

be the coefficient of α in ψ̂ts (α) as defined in (5.18). Intuitively, qk approximates the unconditional probability that
a uniform vertex in a uniformly chosen edge in CMn,JsK has degree k. The main result of the present section is the
following:
Proposition 8.1. Uniformly in s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε],
                                  E P d¯ν

                                           s+1/n,s = k | CMn,Js+1/nK − qk = ōn (1).
     Proposition 8.1 states that the distribution of the current degree of νs+1/n in CMn,JsK is approximately independent
of CMn,Js+1/nK . This result reflects the fact that the macroscopic structure of the graph CMn,Js+1/nK is constrained by
inequalities such as Lemmas 5.4 and 5.5, or informally, laws of large numbers, and therefore that CMn,Js+1/nK only
provides limited additional information about d¯νs+1/n ,s .
     Our proof strategy towards Proposition 8.1 is as follows: Recall that for s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε], with high
probability, νs+1/n is awakened by Step 3. Therefore, its current degree in CMn,JsK is strictly smaller than its original
degree. Therefore, by the law of total probability, on the event that νs+1/n is awakened by Step 3,
                P d¯νs+1/n ,s = k | CMn,Js+1/nK

                   K
                   X
                           P d¯νs+1/n ,s = k | CMn,Js+1/nK , dνs+1/n = ℓ P dνs+1/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK .

               =                                                                                                       (8.2)
                   ℓ=k+1

The benefit of the decomposition of (8.2) is as follows: While the current degree d¯νs+1/n ,s is a priori rather complicated
to work with, the original degree dνs+1/n is more closely related to the graph exploration, as sleeping vertices are
awakened with probability proportional to their original degrees in Step 1 and Step 3. Thus, coming from the graph
exploration, it seems like a more reasonable task to compute P(dνs+1/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK ).
     On the other hand, the probability that the current degree d¯νs+1/n ,s of νs+1/n in CMn,JsK takes on a certain value
given its original degree can be computed by a similar counting argument as in Lemma 5.9. While we provide details
on this counting argument in the proof of Proposition 8.1 in Section 8.4, the main work in this section goes into the
analysis of the conditional distribution of the original degree of νs+1/n . Through a careful examination of the graph
exploration, we prove in Lemma 8.2 that P(dνs+1/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK ) is close to a constant:

      The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


Lemma 8.2. For any ℓ ∈ {0} ∪ [K], uniformly in s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε],
                       E P dνs+1/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK − ℓpℓ e−ts ℓ λ(ts )−1 = ōn (1).
                                                         
                                                                                                                        (8.3)
      In the same spirit as Proposition 8.1, Lemma 8.2 demonstrates that the conditional probability P(dνs+1/n = ℓ |
CMn,Js+1/nK ) is close to the unconditional one. We first prove Lemma 8.2 in Section 8.3 and then Proposition 8.1 in
Section 8.4. The next two sections serve as a preparation towards the proof of Lemma 8.2.
Remark 8.3. In terms of the graph exploration, the conditional probability in Lemma 8.2 still is not easy to compute,
as it conditions on the future graph CMn,Js+1/nK , while seeking information about the past (the last removed vertex
νs+1/n ). One could consider applying Bayes’ rule to exchange the event and the condition. However, the probability
P CMn,Js+1/nK = G | dνs+1/n = ℓ remains challenging to compute, and we still need to prove that dνs+1/n and
CMn,Js+1/nK are approximately independent. So ultimately, this approach would not differ significantly from our
current one.                                                                                                      ■
8.1    The original and a modified graph exploration
      Throughout this section, we fix s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε] and an integer r > 0. The proof of Lemma 8.2, which is
the main technical part in this section, is based on a ‘law-of-large-numbers’ argument. For a start, it might not be too
far-stretched to believe that from the perspective of CMn,Js+1/nK , the original degrees of the last r removed vertices all
behave similarly in the sense that for u ∈ [r],
                                                                                                
                        P dνs+2/n−u/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK ≈ P dνs+1/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK .                              (8.4)
Thus, one could hope that the desired probability can be computed as an average over these last r removed vertices:
                                                   1                                                     
                  P dνs+1/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK ≈ E              u ∈ [r] : dνs+2/n−u/n = ℓ | CMn,Js+1/nK .                 (8.5)
                                                       r
More specifically, at least informally, as we first let n → ∞ and then r → ∞, 1r u ∈ [r] : dνs+2/n−u/n = ℓ should
                                                                                        
converge to a constant by ‘the law of large numbers’. Hence, the right-hand side of (8.5) converges to a constant, and so
does the left-hand side. The proof of Lemma 8.2 is essentially based on this idea.
      In establishing a rigorous version of (8.5), the definition of a slightly modified graph exploration will be instrumen-
tal. For this purpose, we first revisit the graph exploration from [28], as explained in Section 3.4, and make all involved
randomness completely explicit – that is, the i.i.d. exponential life-times and the uniform choices of half-edges in Step
1 and Step 2. Let H be the set of half-edges in CMn . We furthermore set
        • (Eh )h∈H to be the i.i.d. lifetimes of the half-edges used in Step 3, each following an Exp(1) distribution; and
          independently given these lifetimes,
        • (U (h, t, v))h∈H,t∈{0}∪{Ea :a∈H},v∈{active,dead} to be i.i.d. decision variables associated to triples consisting of
          a half-edge h, a potential time t at which Step 1 or Step 2 can be called (initially or after performance of Step
          3), and state v, each following a uniform distribution on [0, 1].
The existence of all these random variables on the same probability space is guaranteed by the Kolmogorov extension
theorem. We imagine that the decision random variables U (h, t, v) associated to half-edge h can be used to set h to
state v at time t. Finally, recall that in the original graph exploration, as time t ≥ 0 evolves, the process successively
goes through Step 1 to Step 3, where Step 1 and Step 2 are performed instantaneously, while it takes an exponential
time to complete Step 3. Thus, in terms of the exponential and decision random variables, the original graph exploration
can be formulated as follows:
  Step 1 At times t when there is no active half-edge (such as at time t = 0), we instantaneously choose the half-edge
          associated to the smallest decision variable among the subset of decision variables that are indexed by
          half-edges that are sleeping at time t, time t and state v = active. We awaken its adjacent vertex and activate
          all its half-edges. The process stops if there is no sleeping half-edge left.
  Step 2 At times t when there is an active half-edge, pick the one associated to the smallest decision variable among
          all decision variables indexed by half-edges that are active at time t, time t and state v = dead. Change the
          status of this half-edge to dead.
  Step 3 Wait until the next half-edge dies because of the time exceeding its lifetime. This half-edge is joined to the one
          killed in Step 2 to form an edge of the graph. If the vertex it belongs to is sleeping, we change the status of
          this vertex to awake and all its remaining adjacent half-edges to active. Repeat from Step 1.
We call η := (Eh , U (h, t, v))h∈H,t∈{0}∪{Ea :a∈H},v∈{active,dead} the decision vector that almost surely uniquely deter-
mines the original graph exploration as described above. Unless stated otherwise, in this section, all variables in the
graph exploration, such as νs , are derived from the original graph exploration determined by the decision vector η.
When we explicitly want to emphasize this, we also write νs (η).


                                                             31
     The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


      We next define a modified graph exploration that is coupled to the original one. While it is rather similar to the
latter, it will be easier to analyze a certain switching procedure in the modified exploration. More specifically, given a
decision vector η, the modified graph exploration will proceed exactly as the original one, using Step 1, Step 2’ and
Step 3, where Step 2’ is the following modification of Step 2:
Step 2’ If the number of awakened vertices in the graph is at least ⌊ns + 2 − r⌋ and at most ⌊ns⌋ at time t, and there
        are active half-edges belonging to the first ⌊ns + 1 − r⌋ awakened vertices, pick the active half-edge associated
        to the smallest decision variable among all decision variables indexed by half-edges that are active at time t
        and belong to one of the first ⌊ns + 1 − r⌋ awake vertices, time t and state v = dead. Change the status of this
        half-edge to dead. Otherwise, pick the active half-edge associated to the smallest decision variable among all
        decision variables indexed by half-edges that are active at time t, time t and state v = dead and change the
        status of this half-edge to dead.
Step 2’ proceeds exactly as Step 2, except when the number of awakened vertices in the graph lies between ⌊ns +
2 − r⌋ and ⌊ns⌋. In the time-window between the awakening of νs+2/n−r/n and νs+1/n (including the steps where
νs+2/n−r/n and νs+1/n are awakened), the aim of Step 2’ is to actively suppress the possibility that half-edges
adjacent to vertices νs−(r−2)/n , . . . , νs , are paired by other decision mechanisms than Step 3. In particular, vertices are
awakened in the same order both in the original and in the modified graph explorations, so that we can unambiguously
use the notation νs for both.
     On a high level, the modified graph exploration facilitates a proof of (8.4) because it is more stable with respect to
small modifications in η in the following sense: Fix η such that Step 1 is not performed between the awakening of
νs+2/n−r/n and νs+1/n in either graph exploration. For u ∈ [r], denote by hu := hu (η) the first dead half-edge of
νs+2/n−u/n with respect to the original graph exploration, so that among the half-edges in {hu : u ∈ [r]}, hr dies first
and h1 dies last. Now given u1 , u2 ∈ [r] with u1 < u2 , suppose that we modify η to η ′ by keeping everything fixed,
apart from exchanging the exponential life-times Ehu1 , Ehu2 . This change has the effect that in the original graph
exploration with respect to η ′ , as Step 1 is not performed, the ⌊ns + 2 − u2 ⌋th awakened vertex is νs+2/n−u1 /n (η).
Consequently, in the next passes through Step 2, in comparison to the exploration with respect to η, other decision
variables are available (first only the ones corresponding to edges adjacent to νs+2/n−u1 /n (η)), and if these are chosen,
the seemingly innocent swap might lead to an avalanche of changes, finally resulting in a significantly altered graph.
On the other hand, in the modified graph exploration, if there are always enough active half-edges, Step 2’ excludes
the possibility to choose from the altered decision variables, so that effectively, the lifetime-exchange only swaps
νs+2/n−u1 /n and νs+2/n−u2 /n along with the neighbors of hu1 and hu2 , but otherwise yields the same graph as the
modified graph exploration with the original life-times.
     Denote by Hr := Hr (η) the set of all half-edges of νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n according to the original and
modified graph explorations. Observe that also the first dead half-edge of νs+2/n−u/n , u ∈ [r], does not depend on the
specific exploration, so that we again use identical notation hu in both cases.
     Next, we identify an appropriate event Os,r which ensures that the original and modified graph exploration
are identical: Define Os,r (Ms,r ) to be the set of all decision vectors η such that, for the original (modified) graph
exploration it holds true that:
      1. All vertices νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n are activated by Step 3;
      2. At the time when the awakening step of νs+1/n finishes, exactly one of its half-edges has been killed for each
         vertex νs+2/n−u/n (for u ∈ [r]);
By definition, the lifetime of h1 , which is the first dead half-edge of νs+1/n , is the maximum of the lifetimes of all
half-edges in {hu : u ∈ [r]}. On the other hand, item 2 in the definition of Os,r (Ms,r ) implies that the lifetime of h1
is smaller than the minimum of the lifetimes of the half-edges in Hr \ {hu : u ∈ [r]}.
Claim 8.1. On Os,r , both the original and the modified explorations perform exactly the same. In particular,
Os,r ⊆ Ms,r .
Proof. By definition, both explorations perform exactly the same until the ⌊ns + 2 − r⌋th vertex has been awakened.
For η ∈ Os,r , this vertex is awakened by Step 3. In the time interval until vertex νs+1/n is awakened, Step 2 and
Step 2’ only differ if there are both active half-edges belonging to the first ⌊ns + 1 − r⌋ awakened vertices and active
half-edges belonging to the already awakened vertices among νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs , and in Step 2, one of the latter
is killed. However, in that case, the adjacent vertex would have at least two killed half-edges at the time when the
awakening step of νs+1/n finishes, which cannot happen on Os,r . Thus, the original and modified graph explorations
agree until vertex νs+1/n is awakened. As they also perform the same afterwards, the claim follows.
     We will now show that Os,r happens with high probability. Thus, with decent probability, the original and modified
graph explorations are the same, and we can transfer any exchangeability properties from the modified graph exploration
back to the original one.

      The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


Claim 8.2. For any integer r > 0,
                                               P(η ∈ Os,r ) = 1 + ōn (1).                                            (8.6)
Proof. By Lemma 5.8 and (7.3),

                                                                                                
       P ∃ u ∈ [r] s.t. at least two half-edges of νs+2/n−u/n are killed when νs+1/n is awakened = ōn (1).
For this, we make the following two observations:
      1. In any iteration of Step 2 after the awakening of νs+2/n−r/n , an active half-edge is chosen uniformly at random
         among all currently active half-edges. As there is at least one active half-edge each time Step 2 is called, at
         any such moment    between the awakening of νs+2/n−r/n and νs+1/n , the number of active half-edges is lower
         bounded by max 1, LJs+2/nK − SJs+2/n−r/nK . As a consequence, for each iteration i of Step 2 after the
         awakening of νs+2n−r/n , by Lemmas 5.8 and 5.10,

         Lemma 5.10,

                                                                                                         

On the event that νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n are activated by Step 3 and Gε,s+2/n holds, B ≥ N r implies that between
the awakening of νs+2/n−r/n and that of νs+1/n , at least N r half-edges were killed in iterations of Step 2. On the
other hand, the number of living half-edges cannot exceed Kn, while on Gε,s+2/n , the number of sleeping half-edges
in the given time frame is always bounded below by cn. Consequently, each time a half-edge is killed in Step 2, with
probability at least c/K it is a sleeping half-edge and a new vertex is awakened. By Chebyshev’s inequality,

               N →∞
Combining (8.7) and (8.12) gives (8.6).
8.2   Switching in the modified graph exploration
     In this section, we define a switching operation on the r first killed half-edges of νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n that
works well with the modified graph exploration. Fix a permutation ρ on [r]. Then for each decision vector η, we define
a new decision vector ϱ(η) by replacing the lifetime of half-edge hu by Ehρ(u) , for u ∈ [r]. Finally, as we are interested
in degrees, let (wu )u∈[r] be a sequence of integers in [K] and Ms,(wu )u∈[r] ⊆ Ms,r be the subset of all decision vectors
such that the original degree of νs+2/n−u/n is equal to wu for all u ∈ [r]. Let CMm  n be the graph obtained through the
modified graph exploration procedure. The following claim then clarifies the effect of this operation on the modified
graph exploration and the degrees of νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n :


                                                            33
     The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


Lemma 8.4. For each permutation ρ on [r] and η ∈ Ms,(wu )u∈[r] ,
      1. ϱ(η) ∈ Ms,(wρ(u) )u∈[r] ;
      2. CMm                                                                                m
             n (ϱ(η)) agrees with CMn (η), except that for u ∈ [r], the neighbor of hu in CMn (η) becomes the
                                   m
         neighbor of hρ−1 (u) in CMn (ϱ(η)).
     As we will show in the following proof, for η ∈ Ms,(wu )u∈[r] , the same half-edges are killed in the modified
graph exploration with respect to η and with respect to ϱ(η), except that when hu ∈ Hr is killed in the modified graph
exploration with respect to η, hρ(u) ∈ Hr is killed in the modified graph exploration with respect to ϱ(η).

Proof. In the following, let ‘step’ refer to Step 1, Step 2’ or Step 3 in the modified graph exploration, where Step 1 is
also counted there are active half-edges remaining. We note that the random variables Eh and U (h, t, v) in the decision
set η are distinct almost surely. Therefore, almost surely, the trajectory of the set of lifetimes of living half-edges with
respect to the number of taken steps is in bijection with the evolution of the set of living half-edges and therefore the
pairing order of the whole graph exploration. The main ingredient in the proof of Lemma 8.4 is thus the following
claim:
Claim 8.3. For η ∈ Ms,(wu )u∈[r] such that all its components are distinct, at any step ℓ, the set of lifetimes of living
half-edges is the same for the modified graph explorations determined by η and ϱ(η).

Proof of Claim 8.3. We use induction on the number of steps ℓ ≥ 0 that have already been taken.
      For ℓ = 0, no step has been taken yet, and the set of lifetimes of living half-edges is {Eh : h ∈ H} in both modified
graph explorations.
      To advance the induction, assume that the claim is true when at most (ℓ − 1) steps have been taken, so that the sets
of lifetimes of living half-edges after the (ℓ − 1)st step are the same in both explorations. If the ℓth step is an iteration of
Step 1, the sets of lifetimes of living half-edges do not change from step ℓ − 1 to step ℓ, since no half-edges are killed
in Step 1. If the ℓth step is an iteration of Step 3, then in both explorations, the half-edge with the smallest lifetime
among all living half-edges is killed and removed from the sets of lifetimes of living half-edges.
      We now consider the case that the ℓth step is an iteration of Step 2’ and emphasize that hu , Hr and νt always refer
to the modified graph exploration with respect to η. Given the set of lifetimes of living half-edges after the (ℓ − 2)th
step, which has been an iteration of Step 3, the following four sets are determined for both η and ϱ(η):
       • The sets of living half-edges: This is due to the fact that the lifetimes stand in (known) bijection to the
         half-edges (even though the bijections may be different for η and ϱ(η));
       • The sets of dead half-edges as the complements of the sets of living half-edges with respect to H;
       • The sets of sleeping half-edges: This is due to the fact that after each iteration of Step 3, the adjacent half-edges
         of vertices with only living half-edges can be unambiguously identified as sleeping.
       • The sets of active half-edges as the complements of the sets of sleeping half-edges with respect to the set of
         living half-edges.
Given this information, we in particular know the sets of awake vertices and next distinguish the cases whether we are
in an epoch between the awakening of νs+2/n−r/n and νs+1/n or not.

      1. Suppose that in the modified graph exploration for η, after step ℓ − 2, there are at most ⌊ns + 1 − r⌋ or at
         least ⌊ns + 1⌋ awakened vertices. Then up to this stage, none or all of the half-edges in {hu : u ∈ [r]} have
         been killed in the modified graph exploration w.r.t. η and thus all or none of their lifetimes are in the set of
         living half-edges after the (ℓ − 2)th step. Since the bijections between a half-edge and its lifetime agree on
         H\ {hu : u ∈ [r]} for both modified graph explorations, we conclude from the induction hypothesis that the
         sets of living half-edges are the same after the (ℓ − 2)th step, and thus that all four sets above are the same.
         Then in the (ℓ − 1)st step, which is an iteration of Step 1, if the set of active half-edges was non-empty in
         the (ℓ − 2)th step, no vertex is awakened, in which case it is immediately clear that all four sets above stay
         the same. On the other hand, if the set of active half-edges was non-empty in the (ℓ − 2)th step, a sleeping
         half-edge is activated according to the value of its decision variable, all of which agree in η and ρ(η). As
         η ∈ Ms,r , the corresponding awakened vertex is not one of νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n in the modified graph
         exploration w.r.t η, and thus the lifetimes of sleeping and active vertices change in the same way. Again, we
         conclude that after the (ℓ − 1)st step, all four sets above agree for η and ρ(η).
         As the sets of active half-edges agree after the (ℓ − 1)st step and the choice of the killed half-edge in step
         ℓ is based on identical decision variables, the same half-edge is killed in both modified graph explorations.
         Moreover, as η ∈ Ms,r , the killed half-edge is not in {hu : u ∈ [r]}, so that it has the same lifetime in η and
         ρ(η). We conclude that the sets of lifetimes of living half-edges are the same after the ℓth step.


                                                              34
     The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


      2. Suppose that in the modified graph exploration for η, after step ℓ − 2, there are at least ⌊ns + 2 − r⌋ and at
         most ⌊ns⌋ awakened vertices. As execution of Step 1 would entail awakening of a new vertex, by item 1 in the
         definition of Ms,r , nothing happens in the (ℓ − 1)st step in the modified graph exploration for η. Moreover,
         by the induction hypothesis, after step ℓ − 2, the sets of lifetimes of living, and therefore also those of dead,
         half-edges agree in the modified explorations w.r.t. η and ρ(η). From this we conclude that every half-edge not
         in {hu : u ∈ [r]} has the same status active, sleeping or dead in both explorations after step ℓ − 2. Therefore, if
         the set of active half-edges w.r.t. η is non-empty, so is the one w.r.t. ρ(η), and nothing happens in the (ℓ − 1)st
         step in the modified graph exploration for ϱ(η) either.
         Moreover, as the set of active half-edges not in {hu : u ∈ [r]} in the ℓth step in both explorations is the same
         and non-empty, in step ℓ, the same active half-edge is chosen according to the value of its decision variable
         and killed in both explorations. As this half-edge is not in {hu : u ∈ [r]}, the same value is removed from
         both sets of lifetimes of living half-edges.
As a consequence, the sets of lifetimes of living half-edges are the same after the completion of the ℓth step.
     From the proof we see that at each step, every half-edge not in {hu : u ∈ [r]} has the same status active,
sleeping or dead in both explorations. Therefore, both explorations perform the same, except when the vertices
νs+2/n−r/n , . . . , νs+1/n are awakened by Step 3. At these times, hu is paired in the exploration w.r.t. η, while hρ−1 (u)
is paired in the exploration w.r.t. ρ(η). This permutes the order in which the ⌊ns + 2 − r⌋th, . . . , ⌊ns + 1⌋th vertices
are awakened and therefore their degrees. It is then very easy to see that ϱ(η) ∈ Ms,(wρ(u) )u∈[r] , and CMm       n (ϱ(η))
agrees with CMm     n (η), except that for u ∈  [r], the neighbor of hu in CM  m
                                                                               n  (η) becomes   the neighbor   of  hρ(u) in
CMm n (ϱ(η)).

9       Fixed-point equations
     Building upon the stability results from Section 7 and the conditional degree analysis from Section 8, we may now
derive the type fixed-point equations. In this sense, the main result of the present section is the following:
Proposition 9.1 (Type fixed-point equations). Fix ε ∈ (0, 1/2−σ(− ln ξ)/2). Then uniformly in s ∈ [ε, 1−σ(− ln ξ)−
ε],

                     ys = 1 − ψ̂ts (xs + ys + us ) − ψ̂ts (xs + ys + vs ) + ψ̂ts (xs + ys ) + ōP (1);                   (9.1)
                     us = ψ̂ts (xs + ys + us ) − ψ̂ts (xs + ys ) + ōP (1);                                              (9.2)
                     vs = ψ̂ts (xs + ys + vs ) − ψ̂ts (xs + ys ) + ōP (1);                                              (9.3)
                     zs ≥ ψ̂ts (ys ) + ōP (1).                                                                          (9.4)
     Throughout the section, to treat all four equations in a unified way, we use the following suggestive notation for
the functions on the right-hand sides of (9.1) to (9.4):
Definition 9.2 (Type functions). Let G denote the set of non-decreasing functions g : [0, 1] → [0, 1] and ∆4 be the
four-dimensional standard simplex. We then define the following three functions Y, U, V : ∆4 × G → [0, 1] by setting
        (i) Y (ζ, g) = 1 − g(x + y + u) − g(x + y + v) + g(x + y) for (ζ, g) ∈ ∆4 × G;
       (ii) U (ζ, g) = g(x + y + u) − g(x + y) for (ζ, g) ∈ ∆4 × G;
      (iii) V (ζ, g) = g(x + y + v) − g(x + y) for (ζ, g) ∈ ∆4 × G;
      (iv) Z (ζ, g) = g(y) for (ζ, g) ∈ ∆4 × G.
                                                                                                                             ♦
     Recall that our proof strategy towards Proposition 9.1 is via Proposition 6.3. Given that we have established the
hold of assumption (6.7) in Section 7, it remains to establish assumption (6.8) (or (6.10)) for w ∈ {y, u, v} (w = z):
                                                               
      P νs+1/n ∈ W (An,s [θ]) | ζs+1/n − W ζs+1/n , ψ̂ts = ōP (1)              (or ≥ ōP (1), respectively).    (9.5)

In order to establish (9.5), in Section 9.1, we relate the conditional probability that νs+1/n assumes a certain type in
An,s [θ] to the types of its neighbors in An,s+1/n [θ] and An,s+1/n [θ]T . We finally prove Proposition 9.1 in Section 9.2.
9.1   Basic and type events
      We first introduce two batches of events, basic and type. The idea is that the type events are (mostly) derived from
different combinations of the basic events, and directly relate to the conditional probability that νs+1/n is of a particular
type in An,s [θ].
Definition 9.3 (Basic and type events). For s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε], define two basic events as follows:
                                                                                  

9.2  Proof of Proposition 9.1
     With Lemmas 9.4 to 9.6 in hand, we are now able to prove Proposition 9.1. It follows directly from the combination
of Lemmas 9.4 to 9.6 that for any W ∈ {Y, U, V } and s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε],
                                                                                 

                          P νs+1/n ∈ Z (An,s [θ]) | ζs+1/n − ψ̂ts ys+1/n ≥ ōP (1).                                 (9.17)
Combining (9.16) and (9.17) with Proposition 6.3 yields the conclusion of Proposition 9.1.

10      Proof of Proposition 4.2: Lower bound on the rank
    Recall that we fix ε ∈ (0, 1/2 − σ(− ln ξ)/2) and let ι = 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε. As outlined, our strategy to
lower-bound the asymptotic rank is a telescoping decomposition according to the graph exploration. In particular, recall
from Section 6.2 that we build on the lower bound
                                   ⌈nι⌉−1
                                    X                                          
              E [rkF (An [θ])] ≥             E rkF (An,s [θ]) − rkF An,s+1/n [θ]    + E [rkF (An,ι [θ])] .          (10.1)
                                   ns=⌊nε⌋
                                                                                                            
In this section, we first derive a lower bound on the individual summands E rkF (An,s [θ]) − rkF An,s+1/n [θ] in
Section 10.1 and then prove Proposition 4.2 in Section 10.2.
10.1    Rank-difference: From fixed-point equations to lower bound
       Already in (6.6), we derived the expression
                                                        
                      E rkF (An,s [θ]) − rkF An,s+1/n [θ] =E [xs + 2ys + us + vs ] + ōn,P (1)                      (10.2)
of the expected rank decrease in terms of the different variable types. Combining (10.2) with the fixed-point equations
(9.1) and (9.2) and the deterministic relation
                                                   αs = xs + ys + vs ,                                              (10.3)
we obtain
                                       h                   i
   E rkF (An,s [θ]) − rkF An,s+1/n [θ] = E αs + 1 − ψ̂ts (αs ) + ōn,P (1) =: E [hts (αs )] + ōn,P (1).            (10.4)

In the last step, we have defined the function ht : [0, 1] → R given by
                                                ht (α) := α + 1 − ψ̂t (α) .                                         (10.5)
Unfortunately, αs in (10.4) is a rather complicated random variable and might not even converge in an appropriate
sense. Nevertheless, the type fixed-point equations in Proposition 9.1 also open the door towards the derivation of a
lower bound on E[hts (αs )], up to an ōP (1) error. To formulate the lower bound, we define the continuous function
Gt : [0, 1] → R given by
                                             Gt (α) := α + ψ̂t (1 − ψ̂t (α)) − 1                                    (10.6)
          ⋆
and let α (t) be the largest zero of Gt in [0, 1]. Then we have the following lower bound on the rank difference:
Lemma 10.1. Fix ε ∈ (0, 1/2 − σ(− ln ξ)/2). For any s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε] and ht as in (10.5),
                          E rkF (An,s [θ]) − rkF An,s+1/n [θ] ≥ hts (α⋆ (ts )) + ōn,P (1).
                                                                
                                                                                                                 (10.7)
    The proofs of Lemma 10.1 and later results in the current section heavily depend on the properties of the functions
Gt and their zeroes. We therefore take a closer look at these now. Let
      1. α0 (t) be the unique zero of the increasing function Ξt : [0, 1] → R given by Ξt (α) = α + ψ̂t (α) − 1. It is
          straightforward to check that α0 (t) is always a zero of Gt .
      2. α⋆ (t) and α⋆ (t) be the smallest and largest (non-necessarily distinct) zeroes of Gt (α) in [0, 1], respectively.
Finally, set
                                        κ := − ln ((ψ̂ ′ )−1 (1) + ψ̂((ψ̂ ′ )−1 (1))).                              (10.8)
Then the following results hold:


                                                             41
       The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


Lemma 10.2 (Properties of Gt and its zeroes). Assume that the probability distribution (pk )k≥0 satisfies Assumptions
2.1, 2.3 and 4.1. Then for t ∈ [0, 1], the function Gt defined in (10.6) has the following properties:
      1. Gt has at most 3 zeroes, and G′t has at most 2 zeroes.
      2. If Gt (α) = 0, then also G(1 − ψ̂t (α)) = 0. Specifically, α⋆ (t) = 1 − ψ̂t (α⋆ (t)) and α⋆ (t) = 1 − ψ̂t (α⋆ (t)).
      3. Gt has 1 or 3 zeroes. If Gt has 3 zeroes, then α⋆ (t) < α0 (t) < α⋆ (t).
      4. For any α > α⋆ (t), Gt (α) > 0.
      5. For t < κ, G′t (α0 (t)) < 0, Gt has precisely 3 zeroes and both G′t (α⋆ (t)) and G′t (α⋆ (t)) are not equal to 0;
          for t > κ, G′t (α0 (t)) > 0 and Gt has only 1 zero; for t = κ, G′t (α0 (t)) = 0 and Gt has only 1 zero. As a
          consequence, for t ̸= κ, Gt and G′t have no common zero while, for t = κ, the only common zero is α0 (κ).
      6. The functions t 7→ α⋆ (t), t 7→ α0 (t) and t 7→ α⋆ (t) are continuous on [0, ∞) and continuously differentiable
          on [0, ∞)\ {κ}.
      7. For a random variable b, if Gt (b) = ōP (1), then also
                                       min {|b − α⋆ (t)| , |b − α0 (t)| , |b − α⋆ (t)|} = ōP (1).

     8. The function Rψt obtains its minimum on [0, 1] for α ∈ {α⋆ (t), α⋆ (t)}.
     The proof of Lemma 10.2 heavily depends on Assumption 2.3 and is given in Appendix E. Given Proposition 9.1
and Lemma 10.2, the proof of Lemma 10.1 is then almost identical to the proof of [26, Proposition 2.14], which is why
we defer it to Appendix G.6.

10.2.1 Proof of Lemma 10.3

                                             ds = d(1 − σ(ts )) = λ(ts ) dts .

11      Proof of Theorem 3.1
     In this section, we prove Propositions 3.2 to 3.4 in their order of appearance. Taken together, they yield Theorem 3.1.
While the subcritical and critical cases Proposition 3.2 and Proposition 3.3 are relatively independent of the previous
sections and given for completeness, removing the finite-degree condition to go from Proposition 4.2 to Proposition 3.4
requires some additional thought.


                                                                   44
       The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


11.1    Proof of Proposition 3.2
     We use the Karp-Sipser leaf-removal algorithm, whose relation to the rank of the adjacency matrix is well-known
in the literature [4, 8, 29], toPestimate the rank in the subcritical and critical cases.
     Given CMn such that k≥0 k(k − 2)pk ≤ 0 and p2 ̸= 1, we aim to compare the rank of the weighted adjacency
matrix An over F to that of the unweighted one over R, for which there is a rank formula available. To this end, we
perform the following peeling process, which is known as the Karp-Sipser leaf-removal algorithm: Choose an arbitrary
vertex of degree at most 1 and remove it, along with its unique neighbor (if existent). Repeat the process until all
remaining vertices have degree at least 2.
     The process stops after at most n rounds. In the end, all trees of the graph will have been removed. We call the
graph that remains after the peeling process the pruned Karp-Sipser core, which is the Karp-Sipser core without isolated
vertices.
                                                                                          0/1        pKS,0/1
     Let ApKSn   be the adjacency matrix of the pruned Karp-Sipser core and let An and An                    be the matrices
                            pKS                                                  0/1      pKS,0/1
obtained from An and An by replacing all nonzero entries by 1. Then An and An                     are the adjacency matrices
of the unweighted version of CMn and its pruned Karp-Sipser core, respectively.
     We next claim that
                                                                                          
                                 rkF (An ) − rkF ApKS      = rkR A0/1      − rkR ApKS,0/1
                                                        
                                                     n                n               n        .                      (11.1)
Indeed, (11.1) holds true because in each step of the peeling process:
       • If a vertex of degree 0 is removed, then both the ranks of the adjacency matrix of the weighted and unweighted
          graph will stay unchanged, since we delete a zero row and a zero column;
       • If a vertex of degree 1 and its unique neighbor are removed, then the rank of the adjacency matrix of the
          weighted and unweighted graph decrease by 2 (see [4] and Figure 1).
      We next show that the size of the pruned Karp-Sipser core is ōP (n). The main idea here is that the local structure
around a uniformly chosen vertex in CMn is tree-like: Let un be a uniform random variable on [n]. By [25, Theorem
4.1] and Assumption 2.1, (CMn , un ) converges locally weakly to the unimodular branching process (G, o) with root
offspring distribution (pk )0≤k≤K . Let Tm be the subset of all rooted trees with depth at most m. Denote by Cn (un )
the connected component of un in CMn . If the (m + 1)- neighborhood of un , i.e., the subgraph induced in CMn by
vertices at distance at most m + 1 from un , is in Tm , then Cn (un ) is contained in the m-neighborhood of un and thus
Cn (un ) is in Tm as well. In other words, the event that Cn (un ) is in Tm is determined by the local structure of the
graph. Then by [25, Theorem 2.15] and the local convergence,
           P ((Cn (un ), un ) ∈ Tm ) =P (the (m + 1)-neighborhood of un in CMn rooted at un is in Tm )
                                      =P (the (m + 1)-neighborhood of o in G rooted at o is in Tm ) + on (1)
                                      =P ((G, o) ∈ Tm ) + on (1).
                                                                                P
Moreover, by [24, Theorem 3.1], in the subcritical and critical case when k≥0 k(k − 2)pk ≤ 0 and p2 ̸= 1, the
unimodular branching process (G, o) with root offspring distribution (pk )0≤k≤K dies out almost surely. Therefore,
                                               lim P ((G, o) ∈ Tm ) = 1.
                                              m→∞
As a consequence,
               lim inf P (Cn (un ) is a finitetree) ≥ lim sup lim inf P ((Cn (un ), un ) ∈ Tm ) = 1,
                   n→∞                                    m→∞     n→∞

i.e., P (Cn (un ) is a finite tree) = 1 + on (1). On the other hand, for each vertex v ∈ [n], if the connected component in
CMn containing v is a tree, then v will be removed in the peeling process. Therefore,
        X
            P (v is in the pruned Karp-Sipser core of CMn ) =nP (un is in the pruned Karp-Sipser core of CMn )
       v∈[n]

                                                         =n − n × P (Cn (un ) is a tree) = on (n).
As a consequence, the expected number of vertices in the pruned Karp-Sipser core, which is equal to the number of
         pKS,0/1
rows of An       (or ApKS
                      n ), is upper bounded by on (n) as well. Hence, (11.1) yields that
                                                                                    

                                                                         

    Finally, by [8, Theorem 2], [1, Theorem 4] and the local convergence of (CMn , un ),

The desired result then follows directly from the combination of (11.2), (11.3) and Markov’s inequality.

11.2    Proof of Proposition 3.3
      In the special case p2 = 1, we remove a small proportion of edges uniformly at random so that the degree
distribution becomes subcritical and Proposition 3.2 applies. More specifically, if we remove a small proportion η of
the edges, the resulting degree distribution satisfies the conditions of Proposition 3.2, while the normalized rank of the
adjacency matrix will only change marginally.

configuration model with a random degree sequence (dη (n, i))n≥1,i∈[n] satisfying Assumption 2.1 with probability
distribution (pηk )k≥0 defined as

11.3.1 Proof of Proposition 11.2 via coupling
     With Proposition 4.2 in hand, it might appear that Proposition 11.2 follows directly through a truncation argument.
Unfortunately, truncating the tail of the probability distribution and re-introducing a finite upper bound on the degrees
might have the effect that the second derivative of the p.g.f. of the resulting probability distribution is no longer
log-concave – even if the original distribution satisfied Assumption 2.3. Thus, in the following proof, to preserve
log-concavity the truncation, we will follow a slightly different route and modify the probability distribution in advance.
     We also utilize the following result that derives uniform convergence from pointwise convergence:
Lemma 11.3. Let (an,k )n,k≥0

Coupling the original and the truncated graph. We next construct a coupling between the original configuration
model CMn with degree sequence (di )i≥0 and limiting degree distribution (pk )k≥0 and a configuration model with
nr,ℓ,k vertices of degree k ≥ 0, where nr,ℓ,k was defined in (11.19). Recall that we use Nk := {i ∈ [n] : di = k} to
denote the (random) set of vertices of degree k in CMn , and nk = |Nk | to denote the cardinality of Nk .
      Let n∧ k := min {nk , nr,ℓ,k } for k ≥ 0. Given (di )i≥0 , for each k ≥ 0, we then choose an arbitrary subset of Nk of

     We next construct CMn from P CMn by adding      the ‘missing’ vertices [n]\ ∪k≥0 Nk∧ one by one according the the
following procedure, e until all k≥0 (nk − n∧  k ) vertices have been added. For each k ≥ 0, suppose that v ∈ Nk \Nk∧
is about to be added to the graph. We then, one after the other, choose the neighbors of its k half-edges in the following
way:
       1. Choose an unpaired half-edge h of v uniformly at random. If there is no unpaired half-edge adjacent to v, the
          process ends.
       2. Choose a half-edge h′ uniformly at random among all half-edges in the graph, excluding the half-edges of v
          that were paired in the previous steps and their half-edge neighbors.
             • If h′ is an unpaired half-edge of v, connect h and h′ .


                                                                50
     The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


           • If h′ is not an unpaired half-edge of v,
              – and there is an unpaired half-edge h′′ of a vertex other than v in the graph, connect h and h′′ ;
              – and all the unpaired half-edges in the graph belong to v, break the connection between h′ and its
                 current neighbor and connect h and h′ . This renders the previous neighbor of h′ unpaired.
         Repeat from 1.
The above construction ensures that CMn is a configuration model, since half-edges are paired uniformly at random.
Indeed, our construction ensures that before a new vertex is added, if there is an unpaired half-edge in the graph, it is
chosen uniformly at random. Hence, when a new vertex v is added, each half-edge, whether already in the graph or
attached to v, has an equal probability of being paired with the currently unpaired half-edge of v. Finally, completely
analogously, we construct CMn,r,ℓ from CM∧     n by successively adding the missing vertices and half-edges. Moreover,
we use the same edge weight matrix Jn in all three graphs CMn , CMn,r,ℓ and CM∧     n.
     We now upper bound the number of edges that were in changed in the processes of constructing CMn from CM∧         n
and CMn,r,ℓ from CM∧   n , respectively. Observe that each time a half-edge of an additional vertex is paired, at most
                                                                                                                 ∧
one previous edge is broken and aP    new one created, so thatPin the end, the number of differing edges in CMn and
                                                      ∧
CMn is bounded from above by 2 k≥0 k (nk − nk ) ≤ 2 k≥0 k |nk − nr,ℓ,k |. Via Remark 3.12, this implies that
the expected rank difference of An and A∧

Proof of Lemma 5.3. Let ε ∈ (0, −(ln ξ)/2). For t ≥ 0, let A(t) = L(t)−S(t) denote the number of active half-edges

E     Proof of Lemma 10.2: Properties of Gt and its zeroes
Lemma 10.2 (Properties of Gt and its zeroes). Assume that the probability distribution (pk )k≥0 satisfies Assumptions
2.1, 2.3 and 4.1. Then for t ∈ [0, 1], the function Gt defined in (10.6) has the following properties:
      1. Gt has at most 3 zeroes, and G′t has at most 2 zeroes.
      2. If Gt (α) = 0, then also G(1 − ψ̂t (α)) = 0. Specifically, α⋆ (t) = 1 − ψ̂t (α⋆ (t)) and α⋆ (t) = 1 − ψ̂t (α⋆ (t)).
      3. Gt has 1 or 3 zeroes. If Gt has 3 zeroes, then α⋆ (t) < α0 (t) < α⋆ (t).
      4. For any α > α⋆ (t), Gt (α) > 0.
      5. For t < κ, G′t (α0 (t)) < 0, Gt has precisely 3 zeroes and both G′t (α⋆ (t)) and G′t (α⋆ (t)) are not equal to 0;
          for t > κ, G′t (α0 (t)) > 0 and Gt has only 1 zero; for t = κ, G′t (α0 (t)) = 0 and Gt has only 1 zero. As a
          consequence, for t ̸= κ, Gt and G′t have no common zero while, for t = κ, the only common zero is α0 (κ).

(multi-)graphs such that (Gn , un ) converges locally weakly to a unimodular Galton-Watson tree with root degree

F.1   Proof of Theorem F.1
     The proof of Theorem F.1 relies on a result by Bordenave, Lelarge and Salez [9] on the normalized matching
numbers of locally weakly convergent sequences of graphs. For a graph G, let ν(G) denote its matching number, i.e.
the maximal size of a collection of pairwise non-adjacent edges in G.
Theorem F.2 ([9, Theorem 2]). Let un be chosen uniformly at random from [n]. Let (Gn )n≥1 = (([n], En ))n≥1 be a
sequence of finite (multi-)graphs such that (Gn , un ) converges locally weakly to a unimodular Galton-Watson tree with
root degree distribution (pk )k≥0 . Then
                                              ν(Gn ) P 1
                                           lim      −→    min Rψ (α),                                               (F.2)
                                          n→∞   n      2 α∈[0,1]
where ψ is the probability generating function of (pk )k≥0 and Rψ is as in (2.3).
     To build the connection between the rank of the symmetric matrices An from Theorem F.1 and the matching
number ν(Gn ) from Theorem F.2, we adapt Lelarge’s idea from [30] to our symmetric setting. We start from the
observation that for any field F, any symmetric matrix A ∈ Fn×n contains a full-rank principal submatrix of dimension
rkF (A).
Lemma F.3 (Any symmetric matrix A has a full rank principal submatrix of dimension rkF (A)). Let A =
(A (i, j))i,j∈[n] ∈ Fn×n be a symmetric matrix with rkF (A) = m. Then there exist 1 ≤ ℓ1 < . . . < ℓm ≤ n
such that B = (A (ℓi , ℓj ))i,j∈[m] ∈ Fm×m is a symmetric matrix of rank m.
Proof. For i ∈ [n], denote by ri = A(i, ) the ith row of A. Since the rank of A over F is m, there exist 1 ≤
ℓ1 < . . . < ℓm ≤ n such that the row vectors (rℓi )i∈[m] are linearly independent. The rank of the submatrix
C = (A (ℓi , j))i∈[m],j∈[n] is m and in particular, C has the same rank as A. Hence, for any i ∈ [n], there exist
ki,1 , . . . , ki,m ∈ F such that
                                                       Xm
                                                  ri =     ki,j rℓj .                                       (F.3)
                                                           j=1

For i ∈ [n], denote by ci = C(, i) the ith column of C. For any i ∈ [n], by (F.3) and symmetry of A,
                                                           m
                                                           X
                                                    ci =         ki,j cℓj .
                                                           j=1

Therefore, the set {cℓj : j ∈ [m]} spans the column space of C. Since the rank of C is m, the symmetric matrix
B = (A (ℓi , ℓj ))i,j∈[m] ∈ Fm×m has rank m = rkF (A), which is the claim.
Proof of Theorem F.1 subject to Theorem F.2. Let now An be as in the statement of Theorem F.1, and denote rkF (An )
by m. By Lemma F.3, there exist indices 1 ≤ ℓ1 < . . . < ℓm ≤ n such that the symmetric submatrix B =
(B (i, j))i,j∈[m] = (An (ℓi , ℓj ))i,j∈[m] ∈ Fm×m has full rank. In particular, its determinant is non-zero, so by the
Leibniz formula for determinants
                                                X             Y
                                       det(B) =     sign (π)      B (i, π(i)) ̸= 0,
                                                 π∈Sm             i∈[m]

where Sm is the set of all permutations
                                 Q         of [m] and sign(π) is the sign of π ∈ Sm . We conclude that there exists a
permutation τ ∈ Sm such that i∈[m] B (i, τ (i)) ̸= 0. Hence, by definition of An , for all i ∈ [m], there is an edge
between ℓi and ℓτ (i) in the graph Gn .
     Now consider the directed graph Lm = (V (Lm ), E(Lm )) = ({ℓi : i ∈ [m]} , {(ℓi , ℓτ (i) ) : i ∈ [m]}), where (a, b)
denotes an edge that is directed from a to b. Since τ is a permutation, each vertex of Lm has out-degree 1 and in-degree
1, even though Lm may have self-loops (corresponding to the fixed-points of τ ). The graph Lm has the following


                                                            58
         The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


structure: We call vertices ℓi with (ℓi , ℓi ) ∈ E(Lm ) 1-polygons. For i ̸= j, if (ℓi , ℓj ) ∈ E(Lm ) and (ℓj , ℓi ) ∈ E(Lm )
(corresponding to the transpositions of τ ), we call {ℓi , ℓj } a 2-polygon. For k ≥ 3, if there exist k different vertices
ℓi1 , ℓi2 , . . . , ℓik such that (ℓis , ℓis+1 ) ∈ E(Lm ) for all s ∈ [k − 1] and (ℓik , ℓi1 ) ∈ E(Lm ), we call {ℓi1 , ℓi2 , . . . , ℓik }
a k-polygon. Since each vertex has out-degree 1 and in-degree 1, the vertices of Lm can be partitioned into polygons as
described.
       The notion of polygons helps to lower bound the matching number ν(Lm ), since each k-polygon contributes
⌊k/2⌋ edges to any maximum matching on Lm . For k ∈ N, denote by sk the number of k-polygons in Lm . Moreover,
for f ∈ N fixed, denote by tf the number of vertices belonging to k-polygons for k ∈ 2N ∪ N≥f . Then by definition,

G.2.1 Proof of (7.7)
      We restrict to the event Pn,s . On this event, An,s+1/n [θ] has a zero column in position νs+1/n , so that νs+1/n is
not frozen in An,s+1/n [θ]. Assume now that i ∈ F An,s+1/n [θ] \F(An,s [θ]). As i is frozen in An,s+1/n [θ], we
first conclude that i ̸= νs+1/n . Next, An,s+1/n [θ] arises from An,s [θ] by zeroing row and column νs+1/n , which we
break into two steps. By Lemma G.2, on Pn,s , replacing a zero row by an arbitrary row cannot unfreeze i, so
                                                                                             
                            i ∈ F An,s+1/n [θ]          =⇒        i ∈ F An,s [θ]J; νs+1/n K .
In particular, there also exists a representation y of {i} in An,s [θ]J; νs+1/n K. Applying this representation y to An,s [θ]
yields
                                           {i} ⊆ supp(yAn,s [θ]) ⊆ {i, νs+1/n }.
Since i ̸∈ F (An,s [θ]) by assumption, we conclude that
                                             supp(yAn,s [θ]) = {i, νs+1/n }.
The just discovered relation {i, νs+1/n } is proper in An,s [θ], since any representation of {i} or {νs+1/n } could be
combined with y to yield a representation of {νs+1/n } or {i}, in contradiction to the assumption that i is not frozen in
An,s [θ].
G.2.2 Proof of (7.8)
                                                                                      
     We restrict to the event Pn,s . Assume that i ∈ F(An,s [θ])\F An,s+1/n [θ] and i ∈         / {νs+1/n } ∪
supp(An,s (νs+1/n , )). The matrix An,s+1/n [θ] arises from An,s [θ] by replacing row and column νs+1/n by a
zero row and column. By Lemma G.2, replacing column νs+1/n by a zero row cannot unfreeze i, so that
                                                                                   
                           i ∈ F (An,s [θ])     =⇒        i ∈ F An,s [θ]J; νs+1/n K .
As we assume that i is frozen in An,s [θ], this implies the existence of a representation y of {i} in An,s [θ]J; νs+1/n K.
Moreover, any such representation y has to use row νs+1/n (i.e., yνs+1/n ̸= 0), as otherwise y would also be a
representation of {i} in An,s+1/n [θ], in contrast to our assumption that i is not frozen in An,s+1/n [θ]. Hence,
multiplying y from the left with An,s+1/n [θ] yields non-zero coordinates in places
                                 supp(yAn,s+1/n [θ]) = {i} ∪ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )).
Thus, {i} ∪ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )) is a relation in An,s+1/n [θ]. It is proper, since it contains i, which is not frozen
in An,s+1/n [θ].

    One-sided firmly frozen variables - (9.8) for W ∈ {U, V }: If νs+1/n ̸∈ U (An,s [θ]), then, by definition, either
νs+1/n is not firmly frozen in An,s [θ]T , or, if this is not the case, it is frozen in An,s [θ] and firmly frozen in An,s [θ]T .

        The asymptotic rank of adjacency matrices of weighted configuration models over arbitrary fields


In the latter case, the symmetry of frailly frozen variables under transposition (see Lemma G.2) implies that νs+1/n is
also firmly frozen in An,s [θ]. We conclude that if νs+1/n ̸∈ U (An,s [θ]), then either νs+1/n is not firmly frozen in
An,s [θ]T or νs+1/n is firmly frozen in An,s [θ]. Again, by a union bound and Lemma G.3,
                                              
               P νs+1/n ̸∈ U (An,s [θ]) , Us
             ≤P νs+1/n not firmly frozen in An,s [θ]T , Fcs + P νs+1/n firmly frozen in An,s [θ], F(tr)s
                                                                                                          

             =ōn,P (1).
    The claim for W = V follows analogously.
    Frailly frozen or two-sided not frozen variables - (9.9): If νs+1/n ̸∈ X (An,s [θ]) ∪ Z (An,s [θ]), then by definition,
νs+1/n is firmly frozen in An,s [θ] or An,s [θ]T . By a union bound and Lemma G.3,
                                                          
        P νs+1/n ̸∈ X (An,s [θ]) ∪ Z (An,s [θ]) , XZs
      ≤ P νs+1/n firmly frozen in An,s [θ], F(tr)s + P νs+1/n firmly frozen in An,s [θ]T , Fs = ōn,P (1).

G.5    Proof of Lemma 9.5
      The first (and main) step is to prove that on the intersection of Z◦s with a sufficiently likely event, the νs+1/n th row
in An,s [θ] can be linearly combined by the other rows of An,s [θ], from which it follows through Lemma G.2 (i) that
νs+1/n is not frozen in An,s [θ]T .
      On Z◦s ∩ Pn,s , supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )) = supp(An,s (νs+1/n , )) and all variables in supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , ))
are firmly frozen in An,s+1/n [θ]. Ideally, to derive the desired linear combination of An,s [θ](νs+1/n , ) by the other
rows of An,s [θ], we would like to take one representation for each i ∈ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )) in An,s+1/n [θ], and
then simply take the An,s (νs+1/n , i)-weighted sum over these representations. Alas, the νs+1/n th column of An,s [θ]
might contain nonzero entries, and it is not clear that for the existing representations, also the entries of column νs+1/n
sum to zero. Therefore, we are looking for representations of i ∈ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )) that expressly do not use
one of the rows in supp(An,s (, νs+1/n )) = supp(An,s (νs+1/n , )), if such representations exist.
      In fact, on Z◦s ∩ Pn,s , since any i ∈ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )) is firmly frozen in An,s+1/n [θ], it is frozen in
An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K, so there exists a representation of i that does not use row i. To take care of the other rows corresponding
to elements of supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )), we define the event
                                                                                                                        
  C = ∀i ∈ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )): i ∈    / F An,s+1/n [θ]Jsupp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , ));K ∆F An,s+1/n [θ]Ji;K .
The event C is sufficiently likely for our purposes, as the combination of (7.19) and Lemma G.2 gives that

By design, on the event C ∩ Z◦s , any i ∈ supp(An,s (νs+1/n , )) is frozen in An,s+1/n [θ]Jsupp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , ));K.
In particular, there exists a representation b of {i} in An,s+1/n [θ]Jsupp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , ));K with bk = 0 for all

Thus, on the event Z◦s ∩ C ∩ Pn,s , any i ∈ supp(An,s [θ](νs+1/n , )) is frozen in An,s [θ]Jνs+1/n ;K. We con-

     On the other hand, since Z◦s ⊆ F(tr)s , by (3.4), νs+1/n cannot be firmly frozen in An,s [θ] on the event

G.6    Proof of Lemma 10.1
      Recall ht : [0, 1] → R, ht (α) = α + 1 − ψ̂t (α) and that α⋆ (t) ≤ α0 (t) ≤ α⋆ (t) denote the zeros of α 7→ Gt (α).

Lemma G.4. For any t ≥ 0,
                                           ht (α⋆ (t)) = ht (α⋆ (t)) ≤ ht (α0 (t)) .                                     (G.18)
                                                                    ⋆                             ⋆
Proof. By items 2 and 3 in Lemma 10.2, α⋆ (t) ≤ α0 (t) ≤ α (t) and ht (α⋆ (t)) = ht (α (t)). The last inequality in
(G.18) follows from the concavity of ht .
Lemma G.5. The following inequality holds for s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε]:
                      hts (xs + ys + us ) = hts (xs + ys + vs ) + ōP (1) = hts (xs + ys ) + ōP (1)
                                                                                                                         (G.19)
                                          ≤ hts (ys ) + ōP (1).
Proof. The first and second equalities in (G.19) follow directly from (9.2) and (9.3).
    Note that xs + ys + zs + us + vs = 1. Then summing up (9.1), (9.2), and (9.3) gives that
                                xs + zs = 1 − (ys + us + vs ) = ψ̂ts (xs + ys ) + ōP (1).                               (G.20)
Then the combination of (G.20) and (9.4) gives
                         hts (xs + ys ) = xs + ys + 1 − ψ̂ts (xs + ys ) = ys + 1 − zs + ōP (1)
                                         ≤ ys + 1 − ψ̂ts (ys ) + ōP (1) = hts (ys ) + ōP (1),
and thus the last inequality in (G.19) follows.
Proof of Lemma 10.1. We aim to show that
                                              hts (αs ) ≥ hts (α⋆ (ts )) + ōP (1).                                      (G.21)
Then Lemma 10.1 follows directly from the combination of (G.21), (9.1), (9.2) and (6.6).
    Define
                  τ̄s = 1 h′ts (xs + ys ) ≥ 0   and η̄s = 1 − τ̄s = 1 h′ts (xs + ys ) < 0 .
                                                                        

Since τ̄s + η̄s = 1, we divide equation (G.21) into two parts as follows:
                                            τ̄s (hts (α⋆ (t)) − hts (αs )) ≤ ōP (1),                                    (G.23)
                                        and η̄s (hts (α⋆ (t)) − hts (αs )) ≤ ōP (1).                                    (G.24)
In the absence of the error terms ōP (1) and under the assumption that τ̄s ≡ 1 (or η̄s ≡ 1), the proof of Lemma 10.1
would amount to an analytic treatment of the properties of hts . Unfortunately, we have to deal with the error terms
and both cases. In the ensuing argument, we therefore fall back upon Taylor’s Theorem with Lagrange Remainder,
Lemma 10.2 and the following two facts:
       (i) For any function g and υ ∈ {0, 1}, if υa = υb, then υg(a) = υg(b);
      (ii) For a family of differentiable functions (gs )s∈[ε,1−σ(− ln ξ)−ε] , if there exists a uniform bound b such that

           [0, 1], a′s = as + ōP (1) ∈ [0, 1] and s ∈ [ε, 1 − σ(− ln ξ) − ε], since |gs (a′s ) − gs (as )| ≤ b |a′s − as |.
